The equation of a parabola with vertex at (0, 0) is y = ax^2.
We know that the focus is at (0, 4), which means the distance from the vertex to the focus is 4 units.

In a parabola, this distance is equal to 1/(4a). So, we can set up the equation:

Solving for a, we get:
a = 1/16

Therefore, the equation of the parabola is:
y = x^2 / 16

To write the equation of a circle with center (5, -2) and radius 5, we can use the general form of the equation for a circle:

(x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2 = r^2

where (h,k) is the center of the circle and r is the radius.

In this case, the center is (5, -2), so h=5 and k=-2. The radius is given as 5, so we can plug in these values:

(x-5)^2 + (y+2)^2 = 5^2

Simplifying the equation gives us:

(x-5)^2 + (y+2)^2 = 25

This is the final answer.

***********************I'd be happy to help you solve this problem. Here's my attempt:

**Problem:** Find the intersection points (vertices) of the line y = x + 3 and the circle x^2 + y^2 = 17.

**Step 1: Understand the equations**

The first equation is a linear equation in two variables, y = x + 3. The second equation is a quadratic equation in two variables, x^2 + y^2 = 17.

**Step 2: Solve the system of equations**

To find the intersection points, we can substitute the expression for y from the first equation into the second equation:

x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17

Expanding and simplifying the equation:

x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0

**Step 3: Solve the quadratic equation**

Now, we have a quadratic equation in one variable. We can solve it using the quadratic formula or by factoring:

2x^2 + 6x - 8 = (2x - 1)(x + 8) = 0

Setting each factor equal to zero and solving for x:

2x - 1 = 0 --> x = 1/2
x + 8 = 0 --> x = -8

**Step 4: Find the corresponding y-values**

Now that we have found the values of x, we can substitute them back into the first equation to find the corresponding values of y:

For x = 1/2:
y = (1/2) + 3
y = 7/2

For x = -8:
y = (-8) + 3
y = -5

**Final Answer:**

***********************{Explanation: To find the equation of a circle passing through points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1), we can use the following steps:
 
 1. The general form of a circle's equation is x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0.
 2. We need to find D, E, and F using the three given points.
 
 To do this, we'll substitute each point into the general equation and create a system of equations:
 
 1. For (1, 1): 1^2 + 1^2 + D(1) + E(1) + F = 0
 
 This simplifies to: 1 + 1 + D + E + F = 0 or 2 + D + E + F = 0 ...
 
 2. For (2, -3): 2^2 + (-3)^2 + D(2) + E(-3) + F = 0
 
 This simplifies to: 4 + 9 + 2D - 3E + F = 0 or 13 + 2D - 3E + F = 0 ...
 
 3. For (5, -1): 5^2 + (-1)^2 + D(5) + E(-1) + F = 0
 
 This simplifies to: 25 + 1 + 5D - E + F = 0 or 26 + 5D - E + F = 0 ...
 
 Now we have a system of three equations with three unknowns (D, E, and F). We can solve this system for D, E, and F.
 
 Using the first equation: 2 + D + E + F = 0
 
 Subtracting it from the second equation: (13 + 2D - 3E + F) - (2 + D + E + F) = 0
 
 This simplifies to: 11 + D - 4E + F = 0 ...
 
 And using the first equation again, we can rewrite D as: D = -(2 + E + F)
 
 Substituting this into the simplified second equation:
 
 11 + (-2 - E - F) - 4E + F = 0
 
 This simplifies to: 9 - 5E = 0 or E = 1.8 ...
 
 Now, substitute E back into D's expression:
 
 D = -(2 + 1.8 + F)
 
 D = -3.8 - F ...
 
 We have one equation with two variables now (D and F), so we can use the third point to create another equation.
 
 For (5, -1): 25 + 1 + (-3.8 - F)(5) + E(-1) + F = 0
 
 Substituting E as 1.8:
 
 26 + 5D - 2.8 + F = 0 or 23.2 + 5D + F - 2.8 = 0 ...
 
 And since we have D in terms of F: 
 (23.2 + 5(-3.8 - F)) + F - 2.8 = 0
 
 This simplifies to: 23.2 - 19 + 4F - 2.8 = 0 or 1.4 + 4F = 0 ...
 
 But we made a mistake here, the correct simplification should be: 
 
 (23.2 - 19) + (-5.8 - 5F) + F - 2.8 = 0
 
 This is: 3.4 - 5.8 - 4F - 2.8 = 0 ...
 
 Now simplify the equation further:
 
 -4.2 - 4F = 0 or -4F = 4.2
 F = -1.05
 
 Substituting F back into D's expression: D = -(3.8 + (-1.05))
 D = -2.75
 
 So now we have values for D and F, which means we can find the value of E by substituting these values into one of our earlier equations.
 
 Using the first equation: 2 + D + E + F = 0
 
 Substituting D as -2.75 and F as -1.05:
 
 2 + (-2.75) + E + (-1.05) = 0 ...
 
 This simplifies to: 4.6 - 3.8 + E = 0 or 0.8 + E = 0
 So the value of E is: E = -0.8
 
 We now have values for D, E, and F. Now we can put these into our circle equation x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0 to get the final equation.
 
 The final equation will be: x^2 + y^2 - 2.75x - 0.8y - 1.05 = 0 ...
 
 So, this is the equation of the circle that passes through the points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1).

"Explanation": "To find the equation of a circle in standard form, we need to know its center and radius. We are given a point (3, -4) that lies on the circle, and the radius is 7. Let's denote the center as (h, k). Since the point (3, -4) is on the circle, we can use the distance formula to find the equation of the circle.

The standard form of a circle's equation is given by: 
(x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2 = r^2

where (h, k) is the center and r is the radius. 

We are given that (3, -4) lies on the circle with radius 7. This means we can substitute x=3, y=-4, and r=7 into the equation:

(3-h)^2 + (-4-k)^2 = 7^2

Now we need to solve for h and k.

We also know that the distance between the center (h, k) and any point on the circle (x, y) is equal to the radius r. Using this fact with our given point (3, -4), we can set up an equation:

√((3-h)^2 + (-4-k)^2) = 7


Simplifying this equation gives us:

(3-h)^2 + (-4-k)^2 = 49

This is a bit complicated to solve directly. Let's try to find the center (h, k) in a different way.


The distance formula tells us that the distance between two points is equal to the square root of the sum of the squares of their differences in x and y coordinates.

Using this with our point (3, -4), we can set up an equation using the distance formula:


√((h-3)^2 + (k+4)^2) = 7


Simplifying this gives us:

(h-3)^2 + (k+4)^2 = 49

We still have two unknowns: h and k. Let's use our point (3, -4) to write another equation.

(h-3)^2 + (k+4)^2 = 49

h^2 - 6h + 9 + k^2 + 8k + 16 = 49


We want to eliminate h and k from the right-hand side, so we'll add the constant terms on both sides:


h^2 + k^2 - 6h + 8k + 25 = 49


Subtracting 25 from both sides gives us:


h^2 + k^2 - 6h + 8k = 24


Now, to get rid of the quadratic terms on the left-hand side, let's try completing the square for h and k.


For h, we'll add (b/2)^2 to both sides, where b is the coefficient of h:


h^2 - 6h + (-3)^2 = 24 - 9


This simplifies to:

|{h-3}|=√15


Simplifying this expression, we get:


h-3 = ±√15


So h is equal to either 3+√15 or 3-√15.

(k+4)^2 = 8

|{k+4}|=±√8

***********************A nice conic section problem!

Here's my attempt to solve it:

**Step 1: Identify the equation**

The given equation is:
x^2/36 + y^2/9 = 1

This is a standard form of a conic section, specifically an ellipse.

**Step 2: Determine the major and minor axes**

In this equation, x^2/36 corresponds to the major axis (horizontal) with length 2a = 6 (since a^2 = 36), so a = 3.
Similarly, y^2/9 corresponds to the minor axis (vertical) with length 2b = 4.6 (since b^2 = 9), but since it's not an integer multiple of the major axis, we can't simply say b is 1 or 2 or another simple value like a.
Let's find out the correct values.

**Step 3: Calculate the axes lengths**

We have two equations:
x^2/36 + y^2/9 = 1

Multiplying through by both denominators to get rid of them:
(36*x^2)/36 + (9*y^2)/9 = 36
Reducing the fraction:
x^2+ y^2 = 36
So, here we have a correct representation of an ellipse with major axis length 6 and minor axis length sqrt(35.8) which can be approximated as approximately 5.99.
**Step 4: Find the eccentricity**

The eccentricity (e) is given by:
e = √(1 - b^2/a^2)

Substituting a = 3, b ≈ 6:
e ≈ √(1 - 36/9)
e ≈ √0.222
e ≈ 0.471

**Step 5: Summarize the results**

We have solved this problem and found that:

* The major axis length is approximately 6 units.
* The minor axis length is approximately 6 units.
* Eccentricity (e) is approximately 0.471.

***********************A nice problem in Conic Sections!

To find the equation of a hyperbola with foci at (±4, 0), we need to use the standard form of the equation of a hyperbola:

(x-h)^2/a^2 - (y-k)^2/b^2 = 1

or

(y-k)^2/b^2 - (x-h)^2/a^2 = 1

The foci are given as (±4, 0), so we can deduce that the center of the hyperbola is at (0, 0). The distance from the center to each focus is 'c', which is equal to 4 in this case.

For a hyperbola, c^2 = a^2 + b^2

We know c = 4, so we can write:

16 = a^2 + b^2

Since we want to find the equation of the hyperbola with foci at (±4, 0), it must be centered at (0, 0) and the transverse axis is along the x-axis. So, the standard form of the equation is:

(x-h)^2/a^2 - (y-k)^2/b^2 = 1

Substituting h = k = 0, we get:

x^2/a^2 - y^2/b^2 = 1

Now, let's find 'a' and 'b'.

We know a^2 + b^2 = 16. Since the foci are at (±4, 0), it means that c^2 = a^2, as the transverse axis is along the x-axis.

So, we can write:

a^2 = 16

Taking square root of both sides:

a = √16

To write the equation of a circle with center (5, -2) and radius 5, I will use the general form of the equation for a circle:

(x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2 = r^2

where (h,k) is the center of the circle, and r is its radius.

Plugging in the given values, we get:

(x-5)^2 + (y+2)^2 = 5^2

Expanding the squared expressions, we get:

x^2 - 10x + 25 + y^2 + 4y + 4 = 25

x^2 + y^2 - 10x + 4y = 0

And that's the equation of the circle!

***********************{Explanation:
First, we need to find the center of the circle that passes through the points (-2, 1) and (6, -3). To do this, we can use the fact that the perpendicular bisector of a chord in a circle passes through its center.

The midpoint of the chord connecting (-2, 1) and (6, -3) is ((-2+6)/2, (1-3)/2) = (2, -1).

Next, we need to find the slope of the line that passes through the points (-2, 1) and (6, -3). The slope of this line is (-3-1)/(6-(-2)) = -4/8 = -1/2.

Now, we can use the point-slope form of a line to find the equation of the perpendicular bisector. Let's call this equation y - (-1) = -(x - 2). Simplifying this equation gives us y + 1 = -x + 2.

To put this in standard form, we'll move all terms to one side and rewrite it as x + y + 3 = 0.

However, this is the equation of a line, not a circle. To get the equation of the circle, we need to find its radius. We can do this by calculating the distance between the center (2, -1) and either of the two points (-2, 1) or (6, -3).

The distance formula gives us sqrt((x2-x1)^2 + (y2-y1)^2). Plugging in the values for the point (-2, 1), we get sqrt((-2-2)^2 + (1-(-1))^2) = sqrt(16+4) = sqrt(20).

Therefore, the equation of the circle with endpoints (-2, 1) and (6, -3) is x^2 + y^2 - 4x - 2y + 19 = 0.}

"Explanation": "To find the equation of a circle tangent to the x-axis and with its center at (3, 4), we follow these steps:

***********************A nice problem in Conic Sections!

Here's the solution:

**Problem:** Find the equation of a hyperbola with foci at (±4, 0) and vertices on the x-axis.

**Step 1: Recall the standard form of a hyperbola**

The standard form of a hyperbola with its center at the origin (0, 0) is:

**(x^2/a^2) - (y^2/b^2) = 1** for a horizontal transverse axis

where **a** and **b** are constants that determine the shape and size of the hyperbola.

**Step 2: Determine the orientation and values of a and b**

Since the foci are at (±4, 0), we know that the hyperbola is centered on the x-axis. This means that the transverse axis is horizontal.

The distance from each focus to the center is **c**, which in this case is equal to 4. We can use the relationship between **a**, **b**, and **c**:

**c^2 = a^2 + b^2**

to find values for **a** and **b**.

**Step 3: Find the values of a and c**

Since we want the vertices on the x-axis, **a** will be equal to the distance from the center to each vertex. Let's call this value **x**.

The equation becomes:

**(4^2/x^2) - (y^2/b^2) = 1**

Simplifying, we get:

**16/x^2 - y^2/b^2 = 1**

Now, recall that **c^2 = a^2 + b^2**, and we know **c** is equal to 4. We can choose a value for **a** (or **x**) that makes the equation simpler.

Let's set **a** equal to **4/c** (which is equivalent to setting **x** equal to 1). Then:

**16/16 - y^2/b^2 = 1**

Simplifying, we get:

**-y^2/b^2 = 0**

This simplifies further to:

**b^2 = ∞**

 Wait! This can't be right. We need a finite value for **b^2**.

Let's try setting **x** equal to **a** (or choosing a different value for **a**) to see what happens.

If we set **x** equal to 4, then:

**(16/16) - y^2/b^2 = 1**

This simplifies to:

**-y^2/b^2 = 0**

Again, this gives us an infinite value for **b^2**, which can't be right.

However, if we set **x** equal to 4 (or **a**) and then substitute it back into the original relationship:

**c^2 = a^2 + b^2**

we get:

**16 = 16 + b^2**

Solving for **b**, we find that:

**b^2 = -16 + 16 = 0**

This gives us a contradiction, since **b^2** can't be equal to 0.

Let's try again with a different value of **x**.

If we set **x** equal to 8 (which is greater than **c**), then:

**(64/64) - y^2/b^2 = 1**

Simplifying, we get:

**-y^2/b^2 = 0**

Again, this gives us an infinite value for **b^2**, which can't be right.

However, if we set **x** equal to 8 and then substitute it back into the original relationship:

**c^2 = a^2 + b^2**

we get:

**16 = 64 + b^2**

Solving for **b**, we find that:

**b^2 = -48**

This gives us another contradiction, since **b^2** can't be negative.

After trying several values of **x**, let's try setting **c** equal to the distance from each focus to the center (which is 4) and then choosing a value for **a** that makes the equation simpler.

If we set **c** equal to 4, then:

**(4^2/x^2) - y^2/b^2 = 1**

Let's try setting **x** equal to 4. Then:

**(16/16) - y^2/b^2 = 1**

Simplifying, we get:

**-y^2/b^2 = 0**

Again, this gives us an infinite value for **b^2**, which can't be right.

However, if we set **x** equal to 4 and then substitute it back into the original relationship:

**c^2 = a^2 + b^2**

we get:

**16 = 16 + b^2**

Solving for **b**, we find that:

**b^2 = -16 + 16 = 0**

This gives us another contradiction, since **b^2** can't be equal to 0.

Let's try setting **c** equal to the distance from each focus to the center (which is 4) and then choosing a value for **a** that makes the equation simpler.

If we set **c** equal to 4, then:

**(4^2/x^2) - y^2/b^2 = 1**

Let's try setting **x** equal to 3. Then:

**(16/9) - y^2/b^2 = 1**

Simplifying, we get:

**y^2/b^2 = (16/9) - 1**

This simplifies further to:

**y^2/b^2 = (7/9)**

Multiplying both sides by **b^2**, we get:

**y^2 = ((7/9)b^2)**

The standard form of a hyperbola with its center at the origin (0, 0) is:

**(x^2/a^2) - (y^2/b^2) = 1**

where **a** and **b** are constants that determine the shape and size of the hyperbola.

To get this equation into standard form, we can rewrite it as:

**(x^2/((7/9)b^2)) - y^2/((7/9)b^2) = 1**

Recall that **c^2 = a^2 + b^2**, and we know **c** is equal to 4. We can substitute the value of **b^2** into this equation:

**16 = a^2 + ((7/9)b^2)**

Simplifying, we get:

**16 = a^2 + (7/9)((3)^2)**

Since we want the vertices on the x-axis, **a** will be equal to the distance from the center to each vertex. Let's call this value **x**.

**(64/((7/9))) - y^2/((7/9)b^2) = 1**

Simplifying further, we get:

**(576/7) - y^2/((7/9)b^2) = 1**

Multiplying both sides by (7/9), we get:

**72 - y^2/b^2 = (7/9)**

Multiplying both sides by **b^2**, we get:

**72b^2 - y^2 = (7/9)(b^2)**

Finally, let's simplify this expression.

72b^2 - y^2 = (7/9)(b^2)

72b^2 - ((7/9)b^2) = 1(y^2)

(64/9)b^2 = y^2

Dividing both sides by **64** we get:

**(y^2)/((64/9)) = b^2**

Simplifying, we get:

**(9/64)y^2 = b^2**

Multiplying both sides by 1 we get:

**(9/64)y^2 = b^2**

Therefore, the final answer is

A hyperbola is a type of conic section that has two separate branches. The equation of a hyperbola can be written in the form (x-h)^2/a^2 - (y-k)^2/b^2 = 1 or (y-k)^2/b^2 - (x-h)^2/a^2 = 1, where (h,k) is the center of the hyperbola and a and b are the distances from the center to the vertices along the transverse axis and conjugate axis respectively.

In this case, the foci are given as (0, ±5) and (0, ±3), which means that the center of the hyperbola is at (0, 2). The distance between each focus and the center is c = 5. Since the foci lie on a line perpendicular to the transverse axis, we know that the hyperbola opens vertically.

The value of c^2 can be used to find the values of a^2 and b^2 using the equation c^2 = a^2 + b^2.

For this problem, c^2 = 5^2 = 25. We are also given that b^2 = 3^2 = 9.

To solve for a^2, we can rearrange the equation to get a^2 = c^2 - b^2 = 25 - 9 = 16.

Therefore, the equation of this hyperbola is (y-2)^2/9 - x^2/16 = 1 or y^2/9 - (x-0)^2/16 = 1.

(x-h)^2/a^2 - (y-k)^2/b^2 = 1

***********************A nice conic section problem!

Here's the solution:

**Problem:** `x^2/4 - y^2/9 = 1`

**Solution:**

To find the focus, vertex, and asymptotes of a hyperbola in standard form, we can follow these steps:

1. Identify the values of `a`, `b`, and `c`:
	* In this case, `a^2 = 4` and `b^2 = 9`.
	* So, `a = √4 = 2` and `b = √9 = 3`.
	* Since it's a hyperbola, we have `c^2 = a^2 + b^2`. Therefore, `c^2 = 4 + 9 = 13`, which means `c = √13`.
2. Find the **focus**:
	* The focus is located at `(0, c)` or `(0, -c)`, depending on whether it's a horizontal or vertical hyperbola.
	* Since `a^2` and `b^2` have opposite signs (positive and negative), we know this is a vertical hyperbola with its axis along the y-axis. So, the focus will be at `(0, c)`.
3. Find the **vertex**:
	* The vertex of a vertical hyperbola is located on the y-axis at `y = ±a`. Since `a = 2`, the vertices are at `(0, -2)` and `(0, 2)`.
4. Find the **asymptotes**:
	* For a vertical hyperbola, the asymptotes have equations `y = ±(b/a)x` or, in this case, `y = ±(3/2)x`.

So, the final answer is:

```
{
  "Final Answer": {
    "Focus": "(0, √13)",
    "Vertex": "[(-2, 0), (2, 0)]",
    "Asymptotes": "y = ±(3/2)x"
  }
}
```

***********************{Explanation:
Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. The hyperbola passes through point (2,3). This gives us a system of equations that we can use to find the equation of the hyperbola.
2. The foci are at (0,±√10), which means that the center of the hyperbola is at (0,0).
3. Since the foci are on the y-axis, the transverse axis of the hyperbola is vertical.

Given this information, we can write the equation of a hyperbola with a vertical transverse axis as:

(y - k)^2 / a^2 - (x - h)^2 / b^2 = 1

where (h,k) is the center of the hyperbola, and a and b are constants that determine the shape of the hyperbola.

Since the point (2,3) lies on the hyperbola, we can plug these values into the equation to get:

(3 - k)^2 / a^2 - (2 - h)^2 / b^2 = 1

We also know that the foci are at (0,±√10), which means that c^2 = a^2 + b^2 = (√10)^2. This gives us:

a^2 + b^2 = 10

Since the center of the hyperbola is at (0,0), we have h = k = 0.

Now, plug in k = 0 into the equation above and solve for a^2:

(3 - 0)^2 / a^2 - (2 - 0)^2 / b^2 = 1
9 / a^2 - 4 / b^2 = 1

We also know that c^2 = a^2 + b^2, which means that we can express b^2 in terms of a^2:

b^2 = 10 - a^2

Now plug this expression for b^2 into the equation above and solve for a^2:

9 / a^2 - 4 / (10 - a^2) = 1
Cross-multiply to get:

9(10 - a^2) - 4a^2 = a^2(10 - a^2)
Simplify to get:

90 - 9a^2 - 4a^2 = 10a^2 - a^4
Combine like terms to get:

-a^4 + 23a^2 - 90 = 0

Factor the left-hand side of the equation:

-(a^2 - 15)(a^2 - 6) = 0

This tells us that either (a^2 - 15) = 0 or (a^2 - 6) = 0. However, since a^2 > b^2 and a^2 + b^2 = 10, we must have a^2 > 5.

Therefore, we discard the solution a^2 = 15 and take a^2 = 6.

This gives us b^2 = 10 - a^2 = 4.

***********************A nice Conic Sections problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

We are given two points: (0, ±5) and (0, ±3). These points lie on the y-axis, so we know that our conic section is a horizontal hyperbola.

The general equation of a horizontal hyperbola with center (h, k) is:

(y - k)^2 / a^2 - (x - h)^2 / b^2 = 1

where 'a' and 'b' are the distances from the center to the vertices along the transverse and conjugate axes, respectively.

We know that the vertices are at (0, ±5), so we can let:

k = 5 or k = -5
a = 5 (since it's the distance from the center to the vertex)

Similarly, the vertices are also at (0, ±3), so we can let:

k = 3 or k = -3
b = 3 (since it's the distance from the center to the vertex)

Since the foci lie on the y-axis and have a distance of 5 units from the center, we know that 'c' is equal to 5. Using the relationship c^2 = a^2 + b^2, we can solve for 'b':

c^2 = a^2 + b^2
5^2 = 3^2 + b^2
25 = 9 + b^2
b^2 = 16

So, we have:

a = 5
b^2 = 16 (or b = ±4)

Now that we have the values of 'a' and 'b', we can write down the equation of the hyperbola. Since we let k = 3 or k = -3 earlier, we'll use k = 3 for our final answer.

Equation of Hyperbola:
(y - 3)^2 / 25 - (x - 0)^2 / 16 = 1

Final Answer: "[(y - 3)^2 / 25 - x^2 / 16 = 1]"

***********************What a nice problem!

Let's break it down step by step:

**Given information:**

1. The point (2, 3) lies on the parabola.
2. The directorixes x = -1.

**Goal:**
Find the equation of the parabola with its focus on the line x = -1.

**Step 1:** Recall the general equation of a parabola with its vertex at (h, k):
(x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)

where p is the distance from the vertex to the focus, and the focus lies on the axis of symmetry.

**Step 2:** Since the directorixes x = -1, the parabola opens left or right. This means its axis of symmetry will be a vertical line (i.e., parallel to the y-axis). Hence, we can rewrite the equation as:
(x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)

**Step 3:** We're given that the point (2, 3) lies on this parabola. Substitute these values into the equation:

(2 - h)^2 = 4p(3 - k)

**Step 4:** Since we don't have any specific information about p or k, let's focus on finding the vertex (h, k). Recall that the vertex is the midpoint between the point and the focus. Given that the directorixes x = -1, the focus must be at (-p, k).

Let's use the fact that the distance between two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is given by:
√((x2 - x1)^2 + (y2 - y1)^2)

The vertex (h, k) lies midway between (2, 3) and (-p, k), so we can set up the following equation:

h = (-p + 2)/2
k = (3 + k)/2

**Step 5:** Now, let's simplify these equations. We'll assume that p is not zero (if it were, the parabola wouldn't be a proper conic section). From the first equation:
-h = -p/2 + 1
h = p/2 - 1

From the second equation:
k = (3 + k)/2
-2k = 3 + k
-k = 3
k = -3

Now, substitute this value of k back into one of the simplified equations to find h:

h = (-p/2 + 1) = p/2 - 1

**Step 6:** Now that we have expressions for both h and k, let's rewrite the parabola equation using these values. However, recall that this is an ellipse of a parabola problem... There are no y's on the left side of the equation (there should be only x's!). We've got two unknowns in our original equation but one can eliminate since it depends upon another.


The correct answer is:
{Final Answer: "y = (1/4)(x + 2)^2 - 3"}

p/2-1=2
P=6.

(h,k)=(-3,-3).
Thus,
x^2=4*3*(y+3)

Multiplying each side by 1/12, we get
(x/6)^2=(y+3)
which can be written as 
y=(1/4)(x + 2)^2 - 3

***********************{Problem: "Find an equation of a parabola that passes through points (2 , 8), (-1 , 3), and (0 , 4)"}

To solve this problem, we will use the general form of a parabola's equation:

y = ax^2 + bx + c

where a, b, and c are constants to be determined.

Since the parabola passes through points (2 , 8), (-1 , 3), and (0 , 4), we can plug these coordinates into the equation to get three equations in terms of a, b, and c:

a(2)^2 + b(2) + c = 8 ... (i)
a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + c = 3 ... (ii)
a(0)^2 + b(0) + c = 4 ... (iii)

Now we will simplify the equations to make them easier to work with:

4a + 2b + c = 8
a - b + c = 3
c = 4

Next, we can use any two of these three equations to solve for a and b. Let's use equations (i) and (ii):

(1) 4a + 2b + c = 8
(2) a - b + c = 3

We know that c = 4 from equation (iii), so we can substitute this into both equations:

(1) 4a + 2b + 4 = 8
(2) a - b + 4 = 3

Now, let's solve these two equations simultaneously.

First, we will subtract equation (2) from equation (1):

4a + 2b - (a - b) = 8 - 3
3a + 3b = 5

Next, we can divide both sides by 3 to simplify the equation:

a + b = 5/3

Now that we have this equation in terms of a and b, we can solve for either variable. Let's solve for a:

a = (5/3) - b

We can now substitute this expression into one of our original equations. We will use equation (i):

4((5/3) - b) + 2b + c = 8

Now, let's simplify the left-hand side:

32 - 6b = 24

***********************What a great problem!

Okay, let's break it down step by step.

First, we know that the conic section passes through two points: (3, 0) and (0, 2). We are also told that it has a horizontal major axis.

Since the major axis is horizontal, the equation of the conic section will be in the form:

Ax^2 + Bxy + Cy^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0

where A, B, C, D, E, and F are constants to be determined.

We can use the fact that the conic passes through (3, 0) and (0, 2) to form two equations:

A(3)^2 + B(3)(0) + C(0)^2 + D(3) + E(0) + F = 0
A(0)^2 + B(0)(2) + C(2)^2 + D(0) + E(2) + F = 0

Simplifying these equations, we get:

9A + 3D + F = 0 ... (1)
4C + 2E + F = 0 ... (2)

Now, since the major axis is horizontal, we know that A ≠ 0. Therefore, we can divide equation (1) by 9 to get:

A + (1/3)D + (1/9)F = 0

Simplifying further, we get:

A = -(1/3)D - (1/9)F ... (3)

Now, substitute equation (3) into the original equation for the conic section:

-(1/3)Dx^2 - (1/9)Fx^2 + Bxy + Cy^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0

Multiply through by 27 to eliminate fractions:

-Dx^2 -Fy^2 + 9Bxy + 27Cy^2 + 27Dx + 27Ey + 27F = 0

Now, substitute the values of x and y from point (3, 0):

-9D(3)^2 - F(0)^2 + B(3)(0) + C(0)^2 + D(3) + E(0) + F = 0
-27D - F = 0

Simplifying further, we get:

F = 27D ... (4)

Now, substitute the values of x and y from point (0, 2):

-A(0)^2 - B(0)(2) + C(2)^2 + D(0) + E(2) + F = 0
4C + 2E + 27D = 0

Substitute equation (4) into this equation:

4C + 2E + 27D = 0 ... (5)

Now, we have a system of two equations with three unknowns. We can eliminate one variable by substitution.

Rearrange equation (3):

F = -9A - 3D

Substitute this expression for F into equation (4):

-9A - 3D = 27D
-9A = 30D
A = -10/3 D

Now, substitute A = -10/3D into equation (5):

4C + 2E + 27D = 0
4C + 2E = -27D

We still have three unknowns. However, we can use the fact that the major axis is horizontal to eliminate one of them.

Recall that the standard form for a conic section with a horizontal major axis is:

Ax^2 + Bxy + Cy^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0

Since A ≠ 0, we know that Ax^2 ≠ 0. Therefore, the coefficient of y^2 must be zero.

Cy^2 = 0
C = 0

Now, substitute C = 0 into equation (5):

4(0) + 2E - 27D = 0
-27D + 2E = 0

Simplifying further:

E = 27/2 D

Now we have a relationship between E and D. We can use this to eliminate E from the original equation.

Recall that we started with:

-(1/3)Dx^2 - (1/9)Fx^2 + Bxy + Cy^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0

We already know that A ≠ 0, so we can simplify this equation by dividing through by -(1/3)D:

-Fx^2/-(1/3)D - 3x^2 - (9B/D)xy + y^2 + 27x + (54/2E)y + F = 0

Simplify further:

-3Fx^2/(-D) - 3x^2 - (9B/D)x^2y + y^2 + 27x + 27Ey/(2D) + F = 0
(1/3F)x^2 - x^2 - 3xy^2/D + y^2 + 27x + 27Ey/(2D) + F = 0

Now, substitute E = (54/2D):

(1/3F)x^2 - x^2 - 3xy^2/D + y^2 + 27x + 81y/2 + F = 0

Finally, recall that we had previously found:

F = -(9A)/-(1/3D)
F = -27A/(-D)

Substitute this expression for F into the previous equation and simplify:

-3Fx^2 + x^2 - (3xy^2/D) + y^2 + 27x + 81y/2 + (-27A/-D)x^2 = 0

Simplifying further, we get:

(-3F+x^2-(-27A/-D)x^2) + (1-x^2/y^2) + (27x+81y/2)=0
(-3F+x^2-((-27A)/D)x^2)+ y^2 - x^2 + 27x + 81y/2 = 0
(-3F+x^2-( (-27A)/D)x^2+y^2)+ (1-x^2/y^2) + 27x+ 81y/2 = 0

Recall that A = -(1/3)D - (1/9)F, and D = (3*F)/(1)

Substitute the values of A and D into this expression for F:

-(-27A/D)x^2 = - (-27((-1/3)- (1/9)F)/((3F)/1))x^2
= -( ( -27 * ((-1/3) + (-1/9)*F))/ 3*F ) x^2 
= -(-27 * (-1/3 - F/9)/3/F)x^2

The common denominator of the numerator is 9:
-(-27 * (-1 - F / 9)/3/F)x^2
= (( -27 * (-9 - F) )/ (3 * 9*F)) x^2 

Combine like terms:
((27 * ( -9 + F ))/ 81*F)x^2

Substitute this expression for the coefficient of x^2 into the previous equation:

(-3F+x^2+((27*( (-9+F))/81)*F)/x^2) + y^2-x^2+(27x+81y/2)=0 

Multiply both sides by 1 to get rid of the denominator:
-3F + x^2 + (27 * (-9 + F)) / (81*F)x^2  + y^2 -x^2 + 27x + 81y/2 = 0

Now combine like terms:
(27*(-9+F))/(81*F) + 1 + y^2 + 27x+ 81y/2= 0

Simplify further:

(-3*F + x^2 + ( (-27 * (-9 + F)) / (81 * F)) ) + y^2 - x^2 + (27x+81y)/2 = 0
(x^2-3F) + (((-27*(-9+F))/(81*F)) +1+y^2-x^2)+ (27x+81y/2)=0

The coefficient of the x^2 term is -3F, which we can factor:
(( -3 * F )x^2) + (((-27*( (-9+F)/ 81*F )) + (1 + y^2 -x^2)) + (27x+81y/2)=0
(-3 * F)x^2 + (-27((-9+F)/(81*F))+ 1+y^2-x^2)+(27x+81y/2) = 0

Now, we can factor out the x^2 term from this expression for the coefficient of x^2:
((-3xF + x^2)((-27((-9+F)/81*F)))+ (1+y^2 - x^2) +(27x+81y/2))=0

Multiply both sides by 1 to get rid of the denominator, and factor out an x^2 term from this expression for the coefficient:
((-3xF + x^2)((-27*(-9/F+F / 81*F))+ (1+y^2-x^2)+(27x+81y/2)=0 

Now combine like terms:
(-3xF + x^2)(((-27 * (-90+ F)/(81 * F)) + y^2 - x^2) )+ (27x+81y/2)= 0

Recall that when we divided both sides by -(1/3D), we got the expression:

-(1/3) Dx^2 - (1/9)*F*x^2 - (3*xy^2/D)+ y^2 + 27*x + (54/E)y + F = 0

Recall that:
E= 54 / 2
D = (3 * F)/(1)

Substitute the values of D and E into this expression for the coefficient of x^2:

(-1/3) Dx^2 - (1/9)*F*x^2 - (3*xy^2/D)+ y^2 + 27x+81y/2+F=0

Combine like terms:
- (1/3 * D)x^2 - (1/9* F ) x ^2 -(3*xy^2 /D) +(y^2-x^2) + 27x+ 81y/2+F =0 

Multiply both sides by -(3F)/(3F) to get rid of the denominators and simplify:
- ((-1 * D * (3 F)) x^2/(-3 *F * 3)) -(( ( -1 * F * 3 )x^2)/((-9)*F)) + ((3* F * (-3 * xy^2/ (-D)))) /((-27) * F * (-D)) +((y^2-x^2)(-3F/(-3F))) + ((27*x * -3 * F) /(-3 * F * 3)) + (81*y/(2* 3)*F*(-3/F)) =0

Recall that D = (3*F)/(1):

- D = 1

***********************{Explanation: The equation x^2/4 + y^2/9 = 1 represents a conic section, which is an ellipse. To find its area, we need to first determine the lengths of its semi-major and semi-minor axes.
The standard form of an ellipse centered at (0, 0) is (x/a)^2 + (y/b)^2 = 1. Comparing this with the given equation x^2/4 + y^2/9 = 1, we can see that a^2 = 4 and b^2 = 9.
Taking the square roots of these values, we get a = ±2 and b = ±3.
The lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes are |a| and |b| respectively. Therefore, in this case, the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes are both equal to 3.
The area of an ellipse is given by A = πab.
Substituting a = 3 and b = 3 into this formula, we get:
A = π(3)(3)
= 9π
}

{Final Answer: "9π"}

***********************{Explanation: First, we need to find the coordinates of the center. The distance from the vertex (-2, 1) to the x-axis is 12, and the distance to the y-axis is 8. Since these distances are equal, the point must lie on a line perpendicular to both axes. Therefore, it lies on the line with equation y = -x + c for some constant c.

The coordinates of the center can be found by plugging in the x-coordinate of the vertex into the equation: (-2, 1) = (c, -(-2) + c). This gives us c = -3. Therefore, the coordinates of the center are (-3, 5).

Now that we have the coordinates of the center, we can find the standard form of the equation of the ellipse. The major axis is horizontal with a length of 12, so the general form of the equation will be ((x-h)^2)/a^2 + ((y-k)^2)/b^2 = 1.

Since the x-coordinate of the center is -3 and the length of the major axis is 12, we can find the value of a: a = 6 (half the length of the major axis).

Similarly, since the y-coordinate of the center is 5 and the length of the minor axis is 8, we can find the value of b: b = 4 (half the length of the minor axis).

Now that we have the values of a and b, we can plug them into the general form of the equation to get the standard form:

((x+3)^2)/36 + ((y-5)^2)/16 = 1

The standard form of the equation is ((x-h)^2/a^2) + ((y-k)^2/b^2) = 1.}

{Final Answer: "((x+3)^2/36) + ((y-5)^2/16) = 1"}

Comparing this with our equation, we can see that a^2 = 4 and b^2 = 9. This means that a = 2 and b = 3.

For a hyperbola centered at the origin with a horizontal transverse axis:

* The vertices are located on the x-axis, symmetrically placed around the center (0, 0).
* The foci are also located on the x-axis.
* The asymptotes are two lines that intersect at the center and have slopes of ±1/a.

Vertices:
The distance from the center to each vertex is 'a'. Since a = 2, the vertices are located at (±2, 0).

Foci:
For a hyperbola with a horizontal transverse axis, the distance from the center to each focus is c = √(a^2 + b^2). In this case, c = √(4 + 9) = √13.

The foci are located at (±√13, 0).

Asymptotes:
For a hyperbola with a horizontal transverse axis and centered at the origin, the equations of the asymptotes are y = ±x/a.

In this case, since a = 2, the equations of the asymptotes are:

y = ±(1/2)x

Therefore, the vertices are located at (±2, 0), the foci are at (±√13, 0), and the equations of the asymptotes are y = ±(1/2)x.

To find the equation of a hyperbola with foci at (0, ±5) and (0, ±3), we need to identify the key components:

* The foci lie along the y-axis, so this is a vertical hyperbola.
* The distance between each focus and the center is 5 units (from 0 to 5 or -5).
* Since the foci are symmetric about the origin, the x-coordinates of their intersection with the transverse axis are both at (0, ±3).

The formula for a vertical hyperbola centered at the origin is y^2 = 4a^2(x^2/a^2), where 'a' is the distance from the center to the vertices along the transverse axis.

Given that the foci lie at (0,±5) and we know the standard form of a vertical hyperbola equation is y^2 = 4p^2 / (x^2/a^2), let's find 'c', which equals half the distance between the foci. So c = |5 - 3| = 2

We also have 'a' that would be equal to c, hence a = 2
To ensure we can derive an equation for our hyperbola, we must calculate the value of p. For any hyperbola centered on (0,0) and with foci at y=±c, where c > a, the value of c equals √(a^2 + b^2), hence c = √[2^2+15]
